--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 1 results:**
- Champion (16918.68) uses 3 states, all Exploration (B=0) with varying turn rates. Key features: black floor detection, neighbor count conditions, and random probability transitions.
- 2nd place (18843.80): Single-state Repulsion - surprisingly good, suggests spreading/repulsion helps.
- 3rd place (18855.41): ZigZag + Exploration + Repulsion combo.
- Bottom performers: pure attraction, GoToColor, FlockCenter - these don't help for aggregation in the target region.

**Mission insight:** This is described as AGGREGATION but the scoring is "outside target region" (the BLUE-bordered strip). So robots need to get to the BLACK-floored strip near BLUE walls. The champion uses black floor detection (C=0) as a transition, which makes sense - when on black floor, behave differently.

**Key observations:**
1. The champion has all Exploration states - random walk to find the target region
2. Black floor (C=0) transitions are important - signals being in the target strip
3. Repulsion (rank 2) works well - spreading robots helps them find the strip
4. Neighbor conditions help coordinate

**Strategy for Round 2:**
1. Copy champion verbatim (elitism)
2. Small mutations of champion (shift probabilities/gains ~10%)
3. Try champion topology with repulsion in some states
4. Try 2-state machines: explore → stop when on black floor
5. Try GoToColor with blue walls (clr=3 for blue?)
6. Mix exploration with stop-on-black-floor logic

**Color mapping:** Blue walls border the target strip. Camera perceives colored objects. Color 3 might be blue. GoToColor blue walls could guide robots there.

Let me design 14 machines:

1. Champion (exact copy)
2. Champion with slight probability tweaks
3. Champion with different turn rates
4. 2-state: Exploration → Stop on black floor
5. 2-state: Repulsion → Exploration with black floor condition
6. 3-state with GoToColor blue + stop on black
7. Champion variant with repulsion replacing one state
8. 3-state exploration with stronger black floor transitions
9. 2-state: GoToColor(blue) + stop
10. Repulsion + stop on black floor
11. 3-state with attraction when many neighbors
12. Champion topology with different parameters
13. Exploration with multiple transitions including blue color perception
14. 4-state variant

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key insight from Round 2:**
The champion (6732.61) is dramatically better than everything else (next best: 15500.95). The champion structure is:
- State 0: GoToColor(blue, clr=3) → if black floor (C=0, p=0.95) go to state 1 (Stop); if sees blue (C=7, l=3, p=0.5) stay in state 0 direction... wait, transition goes to state 1
- State 1: Stop → if gray floor (C=1, p=0.8) go to state 0; if random (C=5, p=0.05) go to state 0  
- State 2: Exploration(rwm=40) → if black floor (C=0, p=0.9) go to state 0; if random (C=5, p=0.1) go to state 1

Wait, let me re-read: destinations are indexed among OTHER states. With 3 states, from state 0 the others are [1,2], so D=0→state1, D=1→state2. From state 1, others are [0,2], D=0→state0, D=1→state2. From state 2, others are [0,1], D=0→state0, D=1→state1.

So:
- State 0 (GoToColor blue): on black floor → Stop (state 1); sees blue → Stop (state 1)
- State 1 (Stop): gray floor → GoToColor (state 0); random → GoToColor (state 0)
- State 2 (Explore): black floor → GoToColor (state 0); random → Stop (state 1)

**Logic**: GoToColor blue guides robots toward blue walls (target strip). When they arrive (black floor), they stop. If they drift to gray (middle), they go back to GoToColor. State 2 (explore) catches robots not seeing blue. This is elegant!

**Strategy**: 
1. Copy champion exactly
2. Exploit: vary the probabilities (0.95→0.85-0.99, 0.5→0.4-0.7, 0.8→0.7-0.9, etc.)
3. Exploit: vary GoToColor color (try clr=1,2,4,5,6 to see if blue=3 is right or another color works)
4. Explore: add a 4th state, try different stop conditions
5. Explore: 2-state version of same logic

The champion uses clr=3 (blue). The target region has BLUE walls. So blue=3 seems correct and crucial.

Key parameters to vary:
- p0x0: 0.95 (black→stop probability) - try 0.85, 0.99, 1.0
- p0x1: 0.5 (sees blue→stop) - try 0.3, 0.7, 0.9
- p1x0: 0.8 (gray→GoToColor) - try 0.6, 0.9, 0.95
- p1x1: 0.05 (random→GoToColor) - try 0.02, 0.1
- p2x0: 0.9 (black→GoToColor) - try 0.8, 0.95
- rwm2: 40 - try 20, 60, 80
- l0x1: 3 (looking for blue) - keep this

Let me design 14 machines:

## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 3:**

The champion (3442.61) is a 4-state machine, dramatically better than 2nd place (4682.39). Key structure:
- State 0: GoToColor(blue/clr=3) → black floor→Stop(s1); sees blue→Stop(s1)
- State 1: Stop → gray floor→GoToColor(s0); random→GoToColor(s0)
- State 2: Explore(rwm=40) → black floor→GoToColor(s0); random→Stop(s1); sees blue→Explore(s2 itself? No - destination 2 from state 2 with 3 states... wait)

Let me re-decode the champion carefully. With 4 states, destination indices are among OTHER states:
- From state 0 (others: [1,2,3]): D=0→s1, D=1→s2
- From state 1 (others: [0,2,3]): D=0→s0, D=1→s2... wait no.

Actually with 4 states, from state i, D indexes into the list of OTHER states in order [0,1,2,3] minus i.
- From s0: others=[1,2,3], D=0→s1, D=1→s2 (but champion says n0x1 D=1 → s2)... 

Champion s0: n0x0 D=1→s1(stop), n0x1 D=1→s1(stop) - both go to stop on black/blue
Champion s1: n1x0 D=0→s0(GoToColor), n1x1 D=0→s0 - both return to GoToColor on gray/random
Champion s2: n2x0 D=0→s0, n2x1 D=1→s1, n2x2 D=2→s3
Champion s3: n3x0 D=1→s1(stop) on black floor

So state 2 (Explore) transitions: black→s0(GoToColor), random→s1(Stop), sees blue→s3(GoToColor)
State 3 (GoToColor blue): black floor→s1(Stop)

This creates a rich multi-path structure where robots can find the blue strip through multiple routes.

**Key findings:**
- GoToColor(blue/clr=3) is the core mechanism
- Stop on black floor (target region) is essential
- Having a redundant GoToColor state (s3) seems to help
- Exploration state as fallback is good
- p=0.95 for black floor detection works well
- p=0.5 for blue color detection works well

**Strategy for Round 4:**
1. Copy champion exactly
2. Exploit: vary champion parameters slightly
3. Try: make state 2 also GoToColor instead of explore
4. Try: add more transitions in key states
5. Try: different exploration rates
6. Try: 4-state with different topology

Let me focus heavily on exploiting the champion structure since the gap from rank 1 to rank 2 is huge (3442 vs 4682).

## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 4:**

The scores are very tightly clustered at the top (3406-3759), with a big gap to rank 12 (4589). This means the champion topology is very robust, and we're in a narrow valley of good solutions.

**Champion structure:**
- S0: GoToColor(blue/clr=3) → black floor→Stop(s1); sees blue→Stop(s1)
- S1: Stop → gray floor→GoToColor(s0); random→GoToColor(s0); **black floor→Explore(s2)** (new! p=0.4)
- S2: Explore(rwm=40) → black floor→GoToColor(s0); random→Stop(s1); sees blue→GoToColor(s3)
- S3: GoToColor(blue) → black floor→Stop(s1)

Key new element vs round 3 champion: S1 has a 3rd transition (black floor → Explore, p=0.4). This allows robots on black floor to sometimes explore instead of always going to GoToColor. This might help spreading within the black strip.

**What's working:**
- The 4-state structure with 2 GoToColor states
- p=0.95 for black floor detection
- p=0.5 for blue color detection  
- Exploration rwm around 20-60
- S1 having 3 transitions (including black→explore)
- S2 having 3-4 transitions

**What to try:**
1. Copy champion exactly
2. Vary the new parameter: p1x2=0.4 (black→explore from stop) - try 0.2, 0.3, 0.5, 0.6
3. Vary rwm2 further (40-60 range seems good)
4. Try adding blue perception to S1 transitions
5. Try adding gray perception to S0 transitions  
6. Try S3 with 2 transitions
7. Try modifying the exploration state's blue detection probability (0.8)

The tight clustering suggests fine-tuning matters. Let me focus on:
- Champion variants with p1x2 varied (0.2, 0.3, 0.5, 0.6)
- Champion with different rwm2 (20, 30, 50, 60)
- Champion with additional transitions
- A few genuinely different topologies for exploration

## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 5:**

Scores 1-11 are remarkably tight (3262-3469), with rank 12 slightly worse (3509), rank 13 notably worse (4012), and rank 14 terrible (23056). The champion added a 4th transition to S1: sees blue→Explore (p=0.7, l=3). This tiny addition improved from ~3407 to 3262.

**Champion structure:**
- S0: GoToColor(blue) → black floor→Stop(s1, p=0.95); sees blue→Stop(s1, p=0.5)
- S1: Stop → gray floor→GoToColor(s0, p=0.8); random→GoToColor(s0, p=0.05); black floor→Explore(s2, p=0.4); sees blue→Explore(s2, p=0.7)
- S2: Explore(rwm=40) → black floor→GoToColor(s0, p=0.9); random→Stop(s1, p=0.1); sees blue→GoToColor(s3, p=0.8)
- S3: GoToColor(blue) → black floor→Stop(s1, p=0.95)

**Key insight:** S1 now has 4 transitions, including "sees blue→Explore" which is interesting - when stopped and seeing blue, sometimes go explore (perhaps to spread within the strip).

**What to vary:**
1. Champion exact copy (required)
2. Vary p1x3 (blue→explore from stop): 0.5, 0.6, 0.8, 0.9
3. Vary l1x3 color (currently 3=blue, try other colors)
4. Add blue perception to S3
5. Vary p2x2 (blue→GoToColor3 from explore): 0.6, 0.7, 0.9
6. Try S2 with 4 transitions
7. Try adding gray floor transition to S0
8. Try different rwm2 with 4-transition S1
9. Try the champion but with S3 having 2 transitions

Since scores are very tight and we're at 3262, focus on fine-tuning.

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

At every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is outside the target region. The score does not depend on how close the robots are to each other.

The arena has two strips at opposite ends. The floor is BLACK in both strips and GRAY in between. The target region is the strip whose bordering walls are BLUE; the walls bordering the other strip are GREEN.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=6  Spiral        -> traces an expanding spiral
            requires --spd<I>  integer in [1..100]  (spiral speed)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=10  FlockCenter   -> moves toward the centre of the group
            requires --fc<I>  integer in [1..100]  (flocking gain)
  B=11  ZigZag        -> moves in a zigzag pattern
            requires --zfr<I>  integer in [1..100]  (zigzag frequency)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
